- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] A lone surrogate (half of a UTF-16 pair with no partner) reads as U+FFFD, the replacement character it displays as, in every text reader, and name() keeps U+FFFD in its place instead of putting the half back. Putting halves back could pair two of them once a mark between them is removed, so the result would hold a character the text never held. U+FFFD is also what UTF-8 encoding, and so every hash, makes of a lone half. Lone surrogates are not refused at the schema boundary: a page's text can hold them, and reading them as displayed keeps the rest of the claim readable. Hard Rules 4 and 5.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] Every reader puts text on the Unicode 15.0 baseline (asBaseline / onBaseline) before it removes any character, subjectTokens and resolveRelative included. For well-formed text this gives exactly the section 11 result. It differs only where an invisible or nonspacing mark sits between two lone halves, which now stay two U+FFFD instead of joining. So 'Q3 re' + U+D835 + U+200B + U+DC29 + 'ort' no longer reads as 'report', and U+D835 + U+200B + U+DFCF + ' min ago' no longer reads as '1 min ago'. Before, Node 22 and Chrome 153 read such a name as 'priya 1' and Chromium 123 as 'priya', under the same inputsHash and receipt id. Checked on 40,000 fuzzed strings (20,000 with surrogates) plus the grading probe: Node 22, Chromium 123 and Chrome 153 give identical results. Hard Rules 4 and 5.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] The unicode fence reads the TypeScript syntax tree, not source text. A RegExp is accepted only with a string-literal flags argument and a pattern that is a literal, or a template built from BASELINE_CLASS members imported from unicode-baseline.ts, generated tables imported by name, or consts declared once in the file built the same way (amount's SPACE, policy-schema's NAME_CHARACTER). A '+' in a pattern is refused. Text the file composes statically (a template, a '+', a const) is checked for class escapes, and a string naming an engine method (Reflect.get(s, 'normalize')) counts as using it. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] A regular expression with the i flag and no u or v flag counts as Unicode case folding when its pattern is not plain ASCII. Without u, ECMAScript still canonicalises characters with the engine's own uppercase mapping. Plain-ASCII i patterns (punycode's ACE prefix) stay allowed. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] The unicode fence reads syntax, and ESLint covers the rest. The core fences refuse globalThis and .constructor, and the type-aware unbound-method rule refuses reading a String method's value (const m = s.match, [s.normalize], Reflect.apply(String.prototype.match, ...)). A data field named like a matcher, such as a receipt's match reasons group, is data. A method name computed at run time and called through Reflect is beyond both checks and is a review matter. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] The fence skips exactly the three tables scripts/gen-unicode-baseline.ts generates (TABLE_FILES) and trusts a table only when one of them is imported by name. A hand-written unicode-table-*.ts file is scanned like any other, and a test fails if the src/core unicode-table-* files differ from TABLE_FILES. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] This supersedes the 2026-10-02 [SEC-1] line that listed policy-schema.ts and schema-text.ts as pending, and item (c) of the [SEC-1] 'What is not covered' line. Both files read BASELINE_CLASS, the fence has no pending list, no src/core file is exempt, and an exemption may not be added. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] The substitution property draws split surrogate pairs only from characters no hole can hold. stripInvisible is a removal step that readers run only after the swap, so on raw text it pairs halves, and a pair equal to a hole's code point would make the substitution inexact. The later-engine and well-formed properties use every split pair. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] No shipped file may contain an ambient declaration (`declare const/let/var/function/class/enum`, `declare namespace/module/global`). This applies in src/core and in every scope that ships (src, entrypoints, src/bus). Such a declaration emits no code, so the name still reads the real global, yet it hides that global from no-restricted-globals. Tests may still declare the globals they inject. Protects Hard Rules 1 and 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The scope analysis shared by attest/no-network, attest/no-act and attest/core-determinism treats a variable whose every definition is ambient as the real global. A `declare const fetch` or `declare const Date` therefore cannot switch those rules off where the syntax ban does not apply. Protects Hard Rules 1, 3 and 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] In src/core, `new Date` takes exactly one argument and never a spread. With type information that argument must be a number or a Date. Text is read only by normalize-time's `instantMs` (ISO fields and Date.UTC), never by the engine's Date parser: an offset-less string parses in the host's time zone, and other text by engine-specific rules. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] src/core reads no `.constructor` in any spelling (member, computed key, 'constructor' string or template) and no `Symbol.toPrimitive`. With type information it reads no computed member of a Date whose key is not written, and no value typed DateConstructor except the global `Date`. `new Date(0).constructor` is the Date constructor with its clock, and a Date's toPrimitive renders local time. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] Temporal is a restricted global in src/core: in the browsers Attest runs in it is a clock and a time-zone database. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The grep test's scanner lives in tests/unit/core/core-scan.ts and stays independent of lint and types. It reads a written key as a member and matches over the whole file, so `Date['now']` and a call split across lines are found. Aliasing (`const D = Date`) is left to lint and the runtime guard, because text alone cannot tell a type position from a value. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] Any type assertion stacked on another is banned in every scope, including through `satisfies` and through one non-null `!`. An assertion on `as const` is exempt because it only widens. `x satisfies T as U` is banned too, which is stricter than needed; no file uses it. Protects the type checker at every boundary (tech_reviewer).
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The test-timeout guard reads a timeout however it is written: positional, `timeout:`, shorthand, a spread of a file-level options object, a quoted or computed key, `vi.setConfig`/`vitest.setConfig`, or any `testTimeout` property. A value it cannot read counts as offending. A test may not shorten the suite-wide bound (qa_tester rule 10: flaky = failing).
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The runtime purity guard decides who made a call from the stack. The innermost frame that is neither the guard nor a dependency (node_modules, Node internals, native or eval'd code) decides. So a dependency that src/core calls (zod running a core refinement) counts as src/core, and a function the harness injects into src/core (an OffsetAt) counts as the harness, as in the extension. A use from src/core is recorded as well as thrown, so a catch in src/core cannot hide it. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The runtime guard accepts `new Date(text)` only for a strict ISO 8601 timestamp with an explicit offset, the only text the IsoTs schema admits. Lint is stricter and allows no text at all. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The runtime guard poisons reads of Intl, Temporal, performance, crypto, fetch, XMLHttpRequest, WebSocket, EventSource, setTimeout, setInterval, setImmediate, WeakRef and FinalizationRegistry. It does not poison `navigator`, which zod reads while preparing a parse, or `process`, which Node and the test runner need; lint and the grep test fence both in src/core. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The runtime guard runs five workloads through the real src/core: every golden scenario, the §2 demo at four instants, a synthetic ledger (200 claims, 2,000 observations, 4 batches, overrides) graded and replayed, a bench gradeBatch slice (400 claims over 10,000 observations), and the text and time API (relative times in US Eastern, every reason's text, punycode, policy errors). Together they run 87.37% of src/core's lines, measured with v8 coverage. The unpoisoned baseline runs in a hook with its own 120 s bound, and test timeouts are not touched. Protects Hard Rules 4 and 12.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] A golden scenario's outcome under the guard includes any error it throws. A scenario failing for an unrelated reason must therefore fail identically with and without the guard. Golden correctness stays the golden suite's job. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C2] The runtime guard's describe blocks carry the `gate` tag, so `vitest run --tags-filter gate` runs it beside the Sprint 1 grep test. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-3/SEC-V1] [1.close] OBS-1: a HealthReport reason is a reason code, never text. Each item matches HEALTH_REASON (lowercase snake_case segments joined by dots, e.g. selector.missing, not_sent_folder) and is capped at SCHEMA_LIMITS.ID (64), down from the old 120-character free-text ITEM cap. health() runs inside the Gmail and Calendar tabs beside message bodies, so a free-text reason could carry mail into adapterHealth and the export. Hard Rule 2; DEGRADED stays visible with a code (Hard Rule 6).
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-3/SEC-V1] [1.close] OBS-1: adapter, observer and extractor ids and versions are one ComponentId token: an ASCII letter or digit followed by letters, digits, '.', '_', '+' or '-', 1 to 64 chars. No space, line break or '@', so no sentence or address fits. Semver pre-release and build suffixes (2.3.1-beta, 1.0.0+build.7) are allowed. One schema serves HealthReport.adapterId/version, Claim.extractor.id/version (which used to accept an empty string), ObserverCoverage.observerId, CoveragePing.observerId and the bus's adapter.claims and observer.observations ids and versions. Hard Rule 2.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-3/SEC-V1] [1.close] OBS-2: a hash-keyed write (observation contentHash, claim rawHash) that repeats a stored hash keeps the earliest observedAt of all sightings. Instants are compared, not strings. An earlier incoming sighting replaces the stored row inside the same rw transaction. A sighting at the same instant written in another offset leaves the stored spelling alone. This makes the §4.1 rule that the earliest observedAt is kept hold in the ledger, so an out-of-order pass cannot make an event already on the calendar verify 'created'. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-3/SEC-V1] [1.close] OBS-4: a repeated hash must be the same fact. A claim or observation that differs from the stored row in anything but observedAt throws LedgerError('id_conflict') and writes nothing. This reuses the existing closed code ('a different fact under an existing hash' sits next to 'a different hash under an existing id') rather than adding one. Every claim field is compared, extractor id, version and confidence, requestId and claimedAt included, so an extractor upgrade that re-reads a stored claim differently fails loud instead of silently keeping either version. The Sprint 3 adapter must report that as DEGRADED with a reason code. A stored observation edited in place under its contentHash makes the honest re-sighting fail loud rather than merge into it. Hard Rule 6.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-3/SEC-V1] [1.close] OBS-3: project() refuses the row with RedactError('invalid_field') when an allowlisted field is held by an accessor (a getter or a setter-only property) or is inherited at any prototype depth, and never invokes or reads it. This covers occurredAt too: the fact is present but held the wrong way, so the error is invalid_field, not missing_occurred_at. Fields the allowlist does not name are never looked at, however they are held. An own data property shadows an inherited one, and an own undefined means absent. A DOM-backed row class with lazy getters would otherwise be stored without its counterparty and could fall to NOT_FOUND with no DEGRADED. Hard Rules 5, 6 (and 2: nothing is read beyond own data properties).
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] Pre-assignments carry the batches they are in force in. Each `fixed` entry of gradeBatch is {observationId, claimId, after, until?}, in force only where after < evaluatedAt <= until. One function, preAssignments(receipts) in src/core/batch-fixed.ts, derives the entries from the stored receipts for the background and for replay. gradeBatch fixes only those in force at its own evaluatedAt, so reading the pre-assignments a moment late cannot make a batch fix a pair its replay does not. I chose instants on the pairs over passing receipts to gradeBatch because golden scenarios state their fixed pairs directly; the golden loader dates them before every scenario instant. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] SUPERSEDES replay's inclusive reading of an override at a batch's very instant. An override releases an observation (FINAL to NOT_FOUND, `until` = its `at`) or takes one (an overridden UNASKED receipt, after max(computedAt, at)) only in batches evaluated strictly after its `at`. One stamped at a batch's evaluatedAt, or after it but read by that batch, is not in force there, on both sides. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] A claim overridden from PENDING to VERIFIED keeps its stored observationIds fixed in every batch after its last grading (computedAt), not only after the override's `at`. Any later batch left the claim out because it had already read the override, otherwise it would have regraded the claim. Leaving the evidence free there let another claim take it FINAL, and the next batch then found one observation fixed to two claims (CandidatesError fixed_conflict), which stops every later batch. Measured: 1 in 3,000 simulated small ledgers under the at-only reading, 0 in 15,000 after. Hard Rules 4 and 6.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] gradeBatch throws BatchError('claim_fixed') for a graded claim named by any pre-assignment, in force or not: a claim with one is FINAL or overridden and is never graded again. A pre-assignment instant that is not an IsoTs throws NormalizeError('invalid_iso') rather than being read as in force or not. Hard Rule 6.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] SUPERSEDES the [1.close] [TECH-C1/TECH-C4] one-orphan-rule entry ('whatever sources the policy lists for its type') and the end of the candidates() entry ('Such a row is graded as an orphan instead'). An orphan must also come from a source the policy lists for its type: the fromRelevantSource predicate candidates use, shared by gradeBatch and replay through isOrphanCandidate. A row the type's coverage never reads neither verifies a claim nor accuses an agent. Under the old reading a claim was NOT_FOUND while the very row it described made an UNASKED accusation against the same agent. Replay reports a stored UNASKED receipt for such a row as orphan/uncovered. Hard Rule 5 (and 4: one predicate).
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] Follow-up to the [1.close] synthetic-world entry (stray-source rows): the 5% of rows from an unlisted source are now asserted to have no receipt naming them. Non-vacuity: stray rows inside a window of their own source appear in 713 of 1,000 small ledgers (asserted above 50) and in every Done-when ledger. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] LedgerSim now reads 10% of claims first at a batch instant, from its own seeded stream. It stamps 10% of overrides at the batch just run, 10% at the next batch's instant, and 10% up to 5 s after it (applied before that batch reads its pre-assignments). Every seeded synthetic ledger changed again; no committed fixture depends on one. Claims read at a batch instant appear in 570 of 1,000 small ledgers (asserted above 50). Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] The calendar.created first-seen comparison is one function, order.ts passesFirstSeen(firstSeenMs, bound), called by candidates(), batch retrieval and grade's guard. candidates() always tracks a candidate's first sighting as a number (memoised per object), which removes a null branch no input could reach. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [TECH-C1/RPL] Orphan discovery reads each type over the union of the windows of the sources the policy lists for it, not of every source: rows from other sources can no longer be orphans, so it no longer reads rows it would discard. Results are unchanged otherwise.
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-4/TECH-C3] [1.close] The score conditions bestScoreGte and bestScoreLt are not applicable to a claim that is never scored against observations: features.ts isAssignable is false for an underspecified claim, a calendar claim without targetAt, or a type with no sources. So no rule order a loaded policy can take lets NOT_FOUND or partial_coverage assert 'nothing matched' of a claim nothing was matched against. Applicability follows isAssignable, not underspecified alone, because §4.3 names exactly these three cases as never scored. Shipped grades are unchanged because no_target, underspecified and no_coverage already come first. (Hard Rule 5)
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-4/TECH-C3] [1.close] firstMatch remembers each rules array's uniform when shapes only for the rules array of a policy loadPolicy returned. policy.ts isLoadedRules checks a WeakSet that the loader fills. Being frozen no longer qualifies an array, because a frozen rule can still read its when through an accessor; every other array is read afresh on each call. This is a second memo keyed on policy-part identity besides claimPlan, and both key only on objects the loader built. (Hard Rule 4)
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-4/TECH-C3] [1.close] The policy loader refuses a thresholds.partial that rounds to 0 basis points, with 'thresholds.partial: must be > 0'. PARTIAL means some evidence matches (§2). At 0, an observation matching nothing would be assigned, named as the claim's evidence, kept out of orphan detection and graded PARTIAL FINAL, and NOT_FOUND could never be reached. One basis point is the smallest partial threshold that loads; the shipped 0.5 is unaffected. (Hard Rule 5)
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-4/TECH-C3] [1.close] Cumulative spend never counts the claim's own id, whatever copy holds it. priorSpendCents skips every prior with the claim's id, and spendIndex subtracts the claim's own entry when an earlier copy of its id lies in the spend window. One claim read at 10:00:10 and again at 10:00:50 has one id (§10), and gradeBatch keeps the activity.claims copy for an id in both lists. Without this rule, one $30 purchase under a $50 ceiling became a permanent OVER_SCOPE against itself. The §6.3 sum is also written out in tests/property/scope-reference.ts without using scope.ts, and the spend properties check both functions against it. (Hard Rule 5)
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-4/TECH-C3] [1.close] Tests now pin compareFractions's fast-path bound (both cross products at most 2^53 - 1). They use explicit cases where the float products round to the same value (2^53 + 1 against 2^53, neighbours near 2^106). They also use properties that draw cross products a few units apart and that split 2^53 + k into factors in every way. Checking only one of the two products against the bound gives the same results and is not a defect: an exact product of 2^53 or more never rounds below 2^53. (Hard Rule 4)
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [SEC-4/TECH-C3] [1.close] spendIndex's run memo is pinned on spendCurrency. One index asked for the same claim under 'USD' and under no spendCurrency must give priorSpendCents's two different answers, and the spend property asks one index under several spendCurrency values in turn. (Hard Rule 4)
- 2026-10-02 [1.close-r2] [docs] [1.close] [docs] check-docs follows the shell's subshells. A `cd` inside `( … )` ends when the subshell closes, also when the subshell spans several lines of one code block, and command substitution `$( … )` is read the same way. A `)` with no subshell open (a case pattern) changes nothing. A `cd` in a brace group `{ …; }` still carries, as it does in the shell. Hard Rule 12: no doc claims a command that does not exist.

A count in the changelog keeps its source next to it (docs_writer rule 1).

It reproduced 36 defects, 8 of them touching Hard Rules 1–4; all 36 are fixed in code with a test that fails without the fix. The rule-level changes are in docs/grading.md v1.6 and the entries above.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [SEC-1] Correction to the [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] lone-surrogate entry, which says U+FFFD is what UTF-8 encoding, 'and so every hash', makes of a lone half. Only a hash of plain UTF-8 text does that: hashText (redact.ts) and sha256Hex of a bare string encode a lone half as U+FFFD, as WHATWG TextEncoder does. Every canonical-JSON hash (inputsHash, contentHash, rawHash, rowKeyHash, receipt ids) escapes a lone half as \uXXXX first (docs/grading.md §10). So two texts that differ only in a lone half read the same in every reader but keep distinct inputsHash and contentHash values. That is harmless: §10 needs identical inputs to hash identically, not texts that read alike. Measured: hashCanonical({cp: 'Priya \uD833'}) begins 175a56303452 and hashCanonical({cp: 'Priya \uFFFD'}) begins 16d0eda367eb, while hashText gives 4212a18b094d for both; tests/unit/docs/lone-surrogate-hashes.spec.ts recomputes all three. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [SEC-1] This supersedes the [1.close-r2] [SEC-1] entry saying a regular expression with the i flag and no u or v flag folds case only when its pattern is not plain ASCII. Without u, i still compares characters by the engine's own uppercase mapping. So a backreference under i (\1–\9, \k<name>, and the legacy octal escapes \1–\377 that share their syntax) folds whatever text the group captured. /(.)\1/i matches U+019B U+A7DC on Node 22 and Chrome 153 but not on Chromium 123, because Unicode 16.0 gave U+019B an uppercase. The unicode fence now refuses a backreference under i. It also refuses any inline modifier group ((?i:…), (?-i:…), (?i-m:…)) whatever the flags, because a group can switch i on where the flags show none: /(?i:\u019B)/u matches U+A7DC on Chrome 153 and is a syntax error on Chromium 123 and Node 22. A plain-ASCII i pattern with no backreference stays allowed (punycode's ACE prefix): without u, a character above U+007F never folds to ASCII. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [SEC-1] The unicode fence reads every file under src/core, however deep, because src/core/tsconfig.json compiles ./**/*.ts. Any file there that is not a .ts source (.js, .mts, .cts, .tsx, a link) fails the fence; the only exception is the top-level tsconfig.json. A relative import of BASELINE_CLASS or a generated table is trusted only when it resolves, from the importing file's directory, to src/core/unicode-baseline.ts or to that table. './unicode-baseline' inside a subdirectory names another module. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [SEC-1] trim() and toLowerCase() are allowed in src/core only as an exact list. Each entry names the file, the function the call is in, its line text and the rule, and must be met exactly once. In normalize-text.ts they are allowed only in asciiName and in subjectTokens' ASCII branch. In unicode-baseline.ts they are allowed only in trimBaseline, lowerBaseline and lowerPoint. Copying an allowed line into another file or function, or a second time into the same function, fails the fence. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [SEC-1] The later-engine, substitution and lone-surrogate properties run every export of normalize-text.ts, normalize-amount.ts and normalize-relative.ts, the only src/core files that may call normalize, and a test fails when an export is not run. The fence and the properties share one list of these files. asciiLower, sameAddress, stopWordKey and isCurrencyCode joined the properties. The lone-surrogate property leaves out stripInvisible, which removes marks from raw text and so can pair two halves. It also leaves out sameAddress, which compares two addresses code point by code point as read (§5.1): two different lone halves make two different addresses, both flagged confusable. A test shows each reason holds. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [SEC-1] BASELINE_CLASS is frozen and every member is a string, so no src/core module can rewrite a Unicode 15.0 class at load time (Object.assign on it throws a TypeError). The unicode fence trusts these members as the 15.0 classes, and unicode-baseline.spec.ts checks that the object is deeply frozen. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [SEC-1] Each unicode fence control holds one probe, so one probe cannot hide another's miss behind the same rule. unicode-fence-pins.spec.ts refuses each engine method alone: called, by a written key, spelled as a key, and destructured. It refuses every Unicode property name, value and alias except ASCII and Cc: every General_Category value, every binary property, Script and Script_Extensions forms, near-miss spellings, and random names. It also checks each case-folding form one at a time. The Final_Sigma property moved unchanged to tests/property/unicode-sigma.property.spec.ts, which keeps the main property file within the 400-line limit. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] The three purity fences on src/core (lint, the grep test, the runtime guard) are built to catch impurity written by accident or through ordinary idioms. Deliberately evasive code is a code-review matter: a name composed from fragments to dodge every check, a reflection chain built for the purpose, or code that blinds or detects the guard. docs/grading.md §14 lists the forms that remain possible, each with an example. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] src/core reads no `constructor` by destructuring (`const { constructor } = /a/`, `{ constructor: C }`). Lint refuses it as an ObjectPattern key, and the grep test refuses `constructor` followed by `:`, `,`, `}` or `=`. A class's `constructor(` is not a hit. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] src/core may read Reflect only as Reflect.get, Reflect.has and Reflect.isExtensible. It may not use Object's getOwnPropertyNames, getOwnPropertyDescriptors, getOwnPropertySymbols, defineProperty, defineProperties, setPrototypeOf, assign, preventExtensions or seal. Aliasing or passing Reflect or Object is refused, and so are `.call`, `.apply`, `.bind`, `__proto__` and any `.prototype` other than Object.prototype. src/core used none of them. The key of Reflect.get and Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor must be written: a string, a template without expressions, or a plain name. A written key is checked like a member name. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] With type information, src/core may not make text from a value whose type may hide a Date: `unknown`, `any`, `object`, `{}`, a type parameter, or arrays of these. That covers String(), new String(), a template, `+`, join and toString. `string & {}` counts as a string. src/core may not use Object.getPrototypeOf, Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor, Reflect.get or Reflect.has on a Date. A Date in a template or after `+` is now reported by attest/core-determinism itself, not only by the general typed rules. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] src/core reads no error stack and no stack API (stack, captureStackTrace, prepareStackTrace, stackTraceLimit, in any spelling) and no import.meta; lint and the grep test both refuse them. Atomics and SharedArrayBuffer are restricted globals in src/core, by lint and by the grep test. The runtime guard does not poison them, because they read no clock, locale or host data. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] The runtime guard is installed, armed, before any src/core module is evaluated. core-poison.spec.ts imports nothing from src/core statically and loads it with import() after installing the guard; the poisoned run installs it from a Vitest setup file. A built-in src/core captures or calls while loading is the guard's twin. A use made while loading is recorded, and the spec asserts there is none. The guard stays installed and is disarmed only while the unpoisoned baseline is computed. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] The runtime guard calls only its own copies of the built-ins it relies on, taken when its module loads, before src/core loads: Error.captureStackTrace, CallSite getFileName, Reflect.apply, construct, defineProperty, getOwnPropertyDescriptor and get, the String, Set and Array methods it uses, JSON.stringify, decodeURIComponent and RegExp exec. It walks arrays by index, not through array iterators. It snapshots the ECMAScript globals and the poisoned names on globalThis, and every built-in constructor, prototype, namespace and iterator prototype; it fails when any of them changed. The rest of globalThis is not compared. It belongs to the host and the test runner: Node's lazily defined globals, the fake `chrome`, zod's registry. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] A Vitest or Vite frame counts as the harness wherever it sits on the stack. Vitest's module runner loads a src/core module's imports, and reads `performance` to time them, from inside that module's own top-level frame. src/core can import neither package. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] An assignment to a poisoned global is kept inside the twin, so the global stays poisoned. Vitest swaps its own timers in and out around each RPC; before this change, the first swap removed the poison from setTimeout and setImmediate for the rest of the file. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] `pnpm test:poison` runs every core, golden and property test under the runtime guard (extension/vitest.poison.config.ts). The guard is installed before each file's src/core modules load. After every test the run checks that no impure use was recorded and no built-in changed. It fails when less than 95% of src/core's lines ran under the guard; it measured 98.05% lines, 97.21% statements, 90.7% branches and 98.11% functions. core-poison.spec.ts and core-poison-controls.spec.ts are left out because they install their own guards. CI runs it after the coverage step. Protects Hard Rules 4 and 12.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] The guard reads the first 16 stack frames and reads the whole stack only when those decide nothing. Each read costs about 3 µs in V8. In the poisoned run it notes no pure uses. In that run only, Date.parse of one ISO 8601 timestamp with an explicit offset, in ECMAScript's own format (milliseconds exactly three digits), passes as pure, as `new Date` of one already did. The replay property's harness parses about 50 million such timestamps; with each one checked, the 1,000-claim replay ran past its 600 s bound, and now it takes about 22 s. Every other Date.parse is still caught, lint still bans Date.parse in src/core, and core-poison.spec.ts catches every Date.parse. Protects Hard Rules 4 and 12.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] The grep test reads a global (Date, Math, Reflect, globalThis, self) through `(X)`, `(0, X)`, `[X][0]`, `(X.name)` and `X!`. A `/` after any property name, after a contextual keyword used as a name, or after a non-null `!` is read as division. The test bans the local-time and locale method names, toString of a `new Date(…)`, reflection on a `new Date`, and Reflect other than get, has and isExtensible. It also bans Object's reflective and mutating members, .call, .apply and .bind, built-in .prototype, __proto__, error stacks, the stack API and import.meta. Forms that need types, such as `String(date)` and a template of a Date, are left to typed lint and the guard. Protects Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] A stacked type assertion is refused, in both ESLint configs, through containers that hand a value on unchanged, up to five deep. Those containers are a conditional's branches, `??`/`||`/`&&`, a sequence, an array or object literal read back at once, a spread, await and `!`. A call or a function in between ends the chain. The typed no-unsafe-type-assertion rule was not chosen: it would also flag two single, unstacked assertions in shipped code (src/core/policy.ts and src/log.ts). Protects type safety at boundaries and Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [TECH-C2] Every test's timeout is checked as it runs. tests/unit/lint/test-timeout-setup.ts asserts task.timeout >= 60 s before each test, in the main run and the poisoned run. Vitest fixes a test's timeout when it collects the test and runs it with exactly that value, so an aliased it, test.extend, suite(…, n), a shadowed, reassigned or mutated constant, a computed key and vi.setConfig are all read as they take effect. The syntactic scan stays, to catch a timeout that restates the 60 s bound. It now follows names bound to it, test, describe or suite. It reads only file-level constants declared once and never written, reports an options argument it cannot read, and reads the computed keys of a setConfig object constant. Hook timeouts are a separate bound and are not checked. Protects Hard Rule 12 (no load-dependent failures).
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [sightings] A stored claim or observation is never rewritten. A repeated contentHash or rawHash whose sighting is at or after the stored observedAt (compared as instants) changes nothing. One whose sighting is earlier throws the new closed code LedgerError('late_sighting') and changes nothing, so the pass that sent it reports DEGRADED. This supersedes the 2026-10-02 OBS-2 entry, under which an earlier incoming sighting replaced the stored row. A batch may already have graded with the stored observedAt, and inputsHash binds the whole claim and each candidate's {id, contentHash, observedAt}. Moving it made FINAL and overridden receipts fail replay, including receipts of other claims, such as the overridden UNASKED u9 through c10. The upsert also reported the move as a plain no-op. For one observer, in-order delivery still makes the first stored row the earliest sighting. Across two tabs, an earlier pass that lands second is refused loudly, never merged. Hard Rules 4, 6.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [sightings] A new claim's or observation's observedAt is set where the row is written. Inside the readwrite transaction that stores it (scope: the table and meta), the stored observedAt is the latest of the sighting, now from src/clock.ts, and the last recorded batch instant + 1 ms (stampObservedAt, src/ledger/batch-instant.ts). A batch records its evaluatedAt with recordBatchInstant(db, evaluatedAt) inside its readwrite transaction, before it reads anything. An instant at or before the last recorded one throws LedgerError('batch_out_of_order') and writes nothing. Every upsert reads the last instant in a transaction over meta, so it serialises with the batch. As a result, no row is stored with an observedAt at or before the instant of a batch that did not read it: a row sighted before a batch but delivered after it is stored after the batch's instant, and replay rebuilds exactly the rows the batch read. The now floor still holds if a batch failed to record its instant; the batch floor holds when the clock goes back. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [sightings] The last batch instant is the meta key lastEvaluatedAt, written only by recordBatchInstant; setMeta refuses that key with invalid_input. A stored value that fails its schema reads as no instant (logged as ledger.row_invalid with the key only), and the next recordBatchInstant replaces it. Refusing every write while that one meta row is corrupt would stop all evidence from reaching the ledger, and the now floor still keeps new rows after any batch already run. Hard Rules 4, 5.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [sightings] A new row is stored no earlier than the instant it is written. So a re-sighting made before an earlier write of the same row landed is earlier than the stored row and is refused with late_sighting. One observer or adapter never triggers this if it sends its next pass only after the previous write is acknowledged and the background writes its messages in arrival order (Sprint 2.7 and 3.6 carry-forwards). Two tabs of one source that sight a new row within one write's latency can trigger it. The cost is one DEGRADED pass that changes nothing in the ledger. A tolerance window would be an unspecified constant, and it could also hide a pass that lands hours late. Hard Rule 6.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [sightings] A claim's extractor (id, version, confidence) is provenance, not content. A re-read under a stored rawHash that differs only in its extractor is the same claim, returns {inserted:false} and changes nothing. The stored claim keeps the extractor that first read it rather than being updated, because a batch may have graded the stored claim and inputsHash covers the whole claim. Every content field is still compared: agent, requestId, type, counterparty, amountCents, currency, subjectHint, claimedAt, claimedAtPrecision, claimedAtEnd, targetAt and sourceKind. So an extractor upgrade that reads a claim differently still throws id_conflict. This amends the 2026-10-02 OBS-4 entry: an adapter repair that reads claims the same way no longer makes every still-visible claim fail loud. Hard Rules 4, 5, 6.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [sightings] Request.conversationId is the vendor's opaque id. It is a token (VENDOR_ID in src/core/schema-text.ts: an ASCII letter or digit, then letters, digits, '.', '_', ':', '+' or '-', at most 128 characters), or empty when the page shows none. A UUID and conv-1 pass; a conversation title or a line of chat does not. Empty stays valid because it carries no content and is what an adapter reports when the page shows no id. These fields must now be one line (oneLineText): items of Grant.declaredScopes and Grant.observedScopes (120 characters), GradeContext.scope.observed (120, non-empty) and Grant.counterpartyAllowlist entries (320). The ledger's MetaEntry.key is a ComponentId token. These were the id-like or list fields still accepting multi-line text into the bus, the indexed requests table, receiptInputs and the export. Request.textHint stays free text, because build_spec §7 allows the first 200 characters of a request. Hard Rule 2.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [sightings] The ledger specs fix Date at 2026-01-01T00:00:00Z with pinClock in tests/unit/ledger/fixtures.ts. Only Date is faked, so IndexedDB's own scheduling runs as usual. The fixed time is before every fixture's observedAt, so a write stores each sighting as given unless a test moves the clock. tests/unit/ledger/live-ledger.ts runs the background's writes and batches over a real ledger. Each batch records its instant and reads the live rows in one readwrite transaction, then grades them with LedgerSim, and the export is replayed. A stored row that moved after a batch, or a row stored before the instant of a batch that did not read it, shows up as a replay mismatch.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [replay] RP3-1 (Hard Rules 4, 5, 7): Batches are stamped strictly in order, and core refuses a batch that is not. `gradeBatch` throws the new `BatchError('stale_evaluated_at')` when a claim pre-assignment's `after` is at or after `evaluatedAt`. A claim pair's `after` is always a grading of that claim. Taken pairs are exempt, because an override stamped after `evaluatedAt` and read by the batch is legal. Why: a second batch at an instant already graded left the first batch's FINAL evidence unfixed. That issued a false FINAL 'likely' UNASKED receipt for an email a claim held, and later stopped batches with `fixed_conflict` (71 of 300 duplicated-instant ledgers mismatched, 14 threw). A same-instant batch with no FINAL evidence to fix is not visible to core; the background's own last-instant check refuses it. The test ledger (LedgerSim) refuses a batch at or before its last. `regrade` still accepts an equal `computedAt` (§8 v1.4, pinned by a test).
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [replay] RP3-2 (Hard Rule 4): A revert never erases what a batch saw. We chose override history over refusing late reverts, because Sprint 4.3 makes overrides reversible. `Receipt.reverted` is lifecycle data: absent, or 1 to SCHEMA_LIMITS.REASONS entries, oldest first. Each entry is {grade, at, revertedAt, previousState, computedAt, observationIds} and never holds the override's reason (Hard Rules 2, 10). `revert` takes the instant it is written (`{event: 'revert', at}`), stamped by the caller inside the transaction that writes it, with `at ≥ override.at`. Every later event keeps the list. `regrade` refuses a receipt computed while a reverted override still held the subject: new `IllegalTransitionError('regrade_held')`, for a claim up to its last revert and for an orphan inside (at, revertedAt]. A new override is never stamped before the last revert. The schema enforces the order and consistency of the list. One revert past the cap is refused with `invalid_result` rather than dropping history (Hard Rule 6). Replay sets `reverted` aside like `override` and `supersededBy`.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [replay] RP3-2 pre-assignments (Hard Rules 4, 6): A reverted override stays in force for the batches evaluated up to its `revertedAt`. An override from PENDING to VERIFIED fixes the evidence it sat beside up to its revert (`until`). It starts from the later of its grading and the revert before it since that grading, because until then an earlier override held the claim and may have fixed nothing. An overridden UNASKED receipt's orphan is taken in (at, revertedAt]. An override from FINAL to NOT_FOUND releases the FINAL evidence in (at, revertedAt] (a `released` span). FINAL evidence released by an override still in force is now an open `released` span instead of `until = at`. So `until` marks only the end of a reverted override from PENDING, and `gradeBatch` refuses (`claim_fixed`) a graded claim named by any pair that has not ended; every refusal from before still holds. A revert fixes evidence again only if no other claim holds that observation then. Each claim's spells are kept in the order they begin (a grading before a revert at one instant, then claim id). A spell that begins at a revert while another claim's kept spell holds the observation is dropped. A spell that begins at a grading is never dropped, so a corrupt ledger still fails with `fixed_conflict`. A claim whose reverted override is still in force at `evaluatedAt` is left out of the batch, like an overridden one: the background uses `gradedAt`, and replay's batch membership uses the same rule.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [replay] RP3-2 claim-less rows (Hard Rule 4): A PENDING claim-less receipt that holds reverted overrides is kept as stored when a batch no longer produces it, because the batches that ran while an override was in force had its orphan taken. A superseded row is never regraded. Known limitation: 71 of 7,753 such receipts (0.92%) stay PENDING after the last batch stopped producing them, on the 5,000-seed differential simulator. Carry-forward to Sprints 3.6/4.3: the background keeps these rows, and the UI must not show one as 'checking' forever.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [replay] RP3-2 known limitation: When an override from FINAL to NOT_FOUND is reverted while another claim holds that evidence, the evidence stays with the other claim. The reverted receipt then shows its computed grade again, so two receipts name one observation, one of them restored by the user's revert. Measured: 15 of 7,277 receipts with a reverted FINAL-to-NOT_FOUND override (0.21%) on the 5,000-seed simulator. Carry-forward to Sprint 4.3: the revert UI must say that the evidence now belongs to another claim, or refuse that revert.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [replay] RP3-4 (Hard Rules 5, 7): The policy loader refuses an `unasked.threshold` that rounds to 0 basis points ('unasked.threshold: must be > 0'). This is the sibling of the partial-threshold rule: at 0, an attribution whose fired signals weigh nothing would still be 'likely', a FINAL accusation resting on nothing. At 1 bp or more, an UNASKED receipt needs a signal with a positive weight.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [replay] RP3-5 (Hard Rule 10): `attest replay` escapes, with printable(), every piece of text it echoes inside its own lines: the file name in the summary's first line and in the 'cannot read' and 'is not JSON' errors, and every reported value. Line breaks (LF, CR, NEL, LS, PS) are escaped too, so a file name cannot forge an output line on stdout or stderr.

- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [docs] Sprint 3.6 carry-forward: BatchError('stale_evaluated_at') and IllegalTransitionError('regrade_held') mean the background passed a batch instant already graded, or a claim a reverted override still holds. - 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] [process] A third read-only verification attacked the round-2 fixes and reproduced 23 defects (10 touching Hard Rules 1–4). Four were design problems now fixed in code and contract: a row's observedAt is stamped where it is stored, strictly after the last batch (meta.lastEvaluatedAt), and an earlier sighting of a stored row is refused (late_sighting) instead of moving it; batch instants are strictly increasing (stale_evaluated_at, batch_out_of_order); a revert keeps the reverted override on the receipt so replay applies it on (at, revertedAt]; Request.conversationId is an opaque id. - 2026-10-03 [1.close-r4] [sightings] SIM-1 (Hard Rule 4): An override's `at` and a revert's instant now get the floor a new row gets. `writeUserEvent(db, write)` (src/ledger/batch-instant.ts, exported from src/ledger/repo.ts) runs `write(at)` inside one readwrite transaction over receipts and meta, with `at` = the later of now (src/clock.ts) and the last recorded batch instant + 1 ms (`stampEventAt`). Why: replay applies an event in exactly the batches evaluated strictly after its instant, and v1.7 stamped events with the clock alone. After a batch, a clock that went back stamped the event before that batch, so replay put it in force for a batch that never read it: on the differential simulator 16 of 6,000 clock-skew seeds mismatched (61 mismatches), and after a revert every later batch of one seed threw CandidatesError('fixed_conflict'). The transaction runs before or after a batch's own (both write meta), as an upsert does, so an event written after a batch reads that batch's instant. Refusing such an event with a new error code was not chosen: the user's override would be lost to a clock step they cannot see, while the floor moves the instant only past the last batch, which replay reproduces exactly. The floored instant still meets transition's `at ≥ computedAt`. An event whose clock went back past the receipt's own last override or revert is still refused by transition (invalid_event); Sprint 4.3 shows that the change was not saved. Measured after the change, same simulator: 6,000 clock-skew seeds (20001–26000), 129,832 receipts, 8,204 events read from a clock stepped back up to 3 h, 0 replay mismatches, 0 unexpected throws; transition refused 156 overrides and 448 reverts whose clock went back past the receipt's own last event. Carry-forward: Sprint 4.3.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r4] [sightings] SIM-2 (Hard Rules 4, 5): An observation keeps its sighting apart from the instant the ledger stored it. v1.7's stamp sets observedAt no earlier than the write and after the last batch, and §4.1's calendar.created rule read observedAt as 'first seen', so an event already on the calendar but written just after a batch verified 'created' (event sighted 13:50, claim 14:02, written 0.5 s after a 14:05 batch: VERIFIED FINAL). Now, when the stamp is later than the sighting, the ledger also stores the sighting as `Observation.sightedAt`. It is immutable like the rest of the row, set only by the ledger (`observationIntegrityProblems` and so `upsertObservation` refuse an incoming one: project never returns it), strictly before observedAt (schema), absent when the two are the same instant (one fact, one spelling), and never part of contentHash. Every reader of a first sighting goes through `firstSeenAt` in src/core/order.ts (sightedAt, else observedAt): candidates(), batch retrieval, grade's foreign_candidate check, the representative of a contentHash, the §7 calendar anchor (attribution windows and UNASKED finality, `anchorAt`) and the ledger's late_sighting. observedAt alone still decides which batches read a row. A candidate's refHash binds sightedAt when present; canonical JSON drops it when absent, so every row stored as sighted, every v1.7 row and every golden file hash and grade as before. The same write now grades as the on-time write (UNVERIFIABLE PENDING, no evidence), and replay stays exact.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r4] [sightings] SIM-2: A claim keeps no sightedAt. Nothing grades a claim's sighting: its observedAt only decides which batches read it (§3), attribution reads it only to ignore a claim first read after evaluatedAt, and every window and adjacency check reads claimedAt, the agent's own time. Storing the sighting would add a field no rule reads (Hard Rule 2) and change no grade. A claim stamped later than it was read is graded one batch later at most.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r4] [sightings] SIM-2: late_sighting now compares an incoming sighting with the stored row's first sighting (sightedAt, else observedAt) instead of its stored observedAt. A sighting between the two tells the ledger nothing new, so it changes nothing and returns {inserted:false} instead of turning the pass DEGRADED. An earlier one is still refused and the row never moves (Hard Rules 4, 6). Claims compare with observedAt as before. On the differential simulator (contract mode, seeds 1–2000) refusals fell from 17,485 to 14,629, every remaining one earlier than the stored first sighting.

Accepted, not fixed: the stored row cannot move (a batch may have graded with it, Hard Rule 4), and acting on the refused sighting would need a new grading input recording when the ledger learned of it. The refusal is loud: the pass is DEGRADED (Sprint 2.7 carry-forward). docs/grading.md §4.1 states the limit before the rule.

- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r5] [sightings] R4-LEDGER-CLAIM-LATE (Hard Rules 4, 6): A re-read of a stored claim is now a no-op however early it was sighted: `upsertClaim` returns {inserted:false} and never throws late_sighting, which is raised for observations only. This supersedes the claim half of the 2026-10-03 [1.close-r3] late_sighting entry and the [1.close-r4] SIM-2 sentence 'Claims compare with observedAt as before'. Why: nothing grades a claim's sighting. Its stored observedAt only decides which batches read it, and it never moves; every window, adjacency and finality check reads claimedAt. So an earlier re-read is no news and loses nothing: a claim read before a batch but stored after it is graded by the next batch, whichever read was stored first. Refusing it turned the adapter DEGRADED for nothing (Hard Rule 6 noise). The most common case was a second tab's read made after the first tab's read but before that read's write landed (the stamp puts observedAt at the write), refused although it was not earlier than any real sighting. id_conflict is unchanged: a re-read that differs in anything but observedAt and extractor is still refused. Observations keep the refusal, because §4.1's calendar.created rule and the §7 anchor read their first sighting. Measured on the differential simulator through the real ledger (seeds 1–1000 contract; 1–3000 skewEvents; 1–3000 all): 0 late_sighting refusals of a claim, 2,822 / 8,405 / 8,912 claim re-reads earlier than the stored observedAt accepted as no-ops with the stored row unchanged, 0 replay mismatches, 0 unexpected throws, 0 invariant violations. Sprint 3.6: an earlier re-read of a stored rawHash returns {inserted:false}, is not a refusal and does not make the adapter DEGRADED.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r5] [sightings] R4-EVENT-ORDER (Hard Rule 4): `writeUserEvent(db, write, { notBefore })` now also stamps an event at least 1 ms after `notBefore`, the receipt's own last event: `lastEventAt(receipt)` (src/ledger/batch-instant.ts, exported from src/ledger/repo.ts), the latest of its computedAt, its override.at and every revertedAt, compared as instants. The stamp is the latest of now, the last batch instant + 1 ms and notBefore + 1 ms. The caller reads the receipt inside one readwrite transaction over receipts and meta that also runs writeUserEvent, so no other event on the receipt can land between the read and the write. A malformed notBefore throws LedgerError('invalid_input') and writes nothing. This supersedes the [1.close-r4] SIM-1 sentence under which an event whose clock went back past the receipt's own last override or revert was refused by transition (invalid_event) and Sprint 4.3 showed that the change was not saved. Why: the user could not revert (or re-override) until the clock caught up, a refusal caused by a clock step they cannot see. The bound only moves an instant later, past an event the receipt already holds, so the replay argument of SIM-1 is unchanged. 1 ms rather than the event's own instant: the receipt's events stay strictly ordered. Measured on the differential simulator with every event bounded: skewEvents seeds 1–3000 (72,377 receipts, 4,229 events read from a clock that went back) and all seeds 1–3000 (124,785 receipts, 4,237 such events, 28,680 burst steps) gave 0 invalid_event refusals (under [1.close-r4]: 53 overrides and 233 reverts; 75 and 260), 0 replay mismatches, 0 unexpected throws and 0 invariant violations. Control without the bound, seeds 1–1000: 22 overrides and 85 reverts refused, 0 mismatches. Carry-forward: Sprint 4.3 passes the bound.

- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r5] [fences] R4-FENCE-1, R4-FENCE-2 (Hard Rules 1, 4): The globals src/core may read are now an allowlist, not a list of bans. CORE_ALLOWED_GLOBALS (eslint-rules/core-names.js) is TypeScript's ES2023 lib values less CORE_RESTRICTED_GLOBAL_NAMES and CORE_UNSAFE_BUILTINS (eval, Function, Proxy, escape, unescape), and core-purity-allowlist.spec.ts derives it again from the lib and fails when the two differ. The new lint rule attest/core-globals and the grep test report every other global read in src/core, unless the file declares or imports the name. Why: `new Image()` and `clientInformation` passed every fence because no list named them, and a longer list would miss the next host name. The 22 globals src/core reads today are all on the allowlist.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r5] [fences] R4-FENCE-1 (Hard Rules 1, 4): In src/core, lint refuses `@ts-expect-error`, `@ts-ignore` and `@ts-nocheck` (ban-ts-comment), and every triple-slash reference (triple-slash-reference). The grep test refuses the same in any comment, with the pragma names matched in any letter case. Why: the ES2023-only lib of src/core/tsconfig.json is what makes a host name fail the typecheck, and each of these switches that check off. Outside src/core, strictTypeChecked still allows a `@ts-expect-error` with a description.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r5] [fences] R4-FENCE-1, R4-FENCE-2 (Hard Rules 1, 4): clientInformation, Image, Audio, FontFace, Worker, SharedWorker, Notification, BroadcastChannel, open, opener, parent, top, frames, postMessage and navigation join CORE_RESTRICTED_GLOBAL_NAMES. So no-restricted-globals names them, and the runtime guard poisons them. Why: the guard poisons by name and cannot hold an allowlist, since it cannot poison a global the host does not have unless it is named. A host global on no list still passes the guard; lint, the grep test and the typecheck refuse it (docs/grading.md §14 item 9(h)). The other ES2023 built-ins src/core may not use (eval, Function) stay unpoisoned, because zod reads Function while src/core builds a schema.
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r5] [fences] Hard Rule 4: In src/core, RegExp may be read only as `new RegExp(…)` or `x instanceof RegExp`, in attest/core-determinism and in the grep test. Why: RegExp's legacy statics (`RegExp.lastMatch`, `RegExp.$1`, `RegExp.input`, `RegExp.leftContext`) hold the last match any code in the realm made, the caller's included, so reading one would make a grade depend on what ran before. An alias, a member, a destructuring or passing RegExp to Reflect.get reaches them. src/core only ever writes `new RegExp(…)`. The guard does not poison these statics (docs/grading.md §14 item 9(h)).
- 2026-10-03 [1.close-r5] [process] The Sprint 1 close converged after five rounds of fix-then-verify: 63 review gaps, then 36, 23, 4 and 4 defects found by read-only verifiers attacking each round's fixes, every one fixed in code with a test that fails without it, except the documented limits (docs/grading.md §4.1 two-tab sighting, §14 item 9 deliberately evasive src/core code). The last check found only a stale sentence in a sprint note. Built bundles are also scanned for `new Image(` and `new Audio(` beacons (tests/e2e/built-bundle.spec.ts), so a Window-context beacon is caught in the shipped build too (Hard Rule 1).
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Manifest host_permissions are exactly https://mail.google.com/* and https://calendar.google.com/*, read from src/config.ts OBSERVER_HOSTS so one list feeds both the manifest and each observer's `hosts`. No optional hosts, no content scripts yet (2.7 adds them); permissions stay storage, alarms and sidePanel. tests/unit/manifest-hosts.spec.ts pins the source and tests/e2e/built-bundle.spec.ts pins the shipped manifest — Hard Rule 8
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Observer contract: `Observer extends Adapter<RawRow>` (build_spec §7 `Adapter<T>`). extract() returns raw rows {type, rowKey?, fields}, and only the skeleton turns them into Observations, through redact.project. health() returns a HealthCheck (OK, or DEGRADED with at least one reason code) instead of a full HealthReport; the skeleton adds adapterId, version and checkedAt. Why: health() stays pure over the DOM with no clock read, and id and version cannot disagree with the observer's own — Hard Rules 3, 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] One observer reads one ObservationSource and sends one coverage stream. Gmail Sent and Gmail receipts run as two observers, each with its own health and coverage. Why: coverage is per source (docs/grading.md §3, §6.1), and a working receipt parser must not stand in for an unread Sent list — Hard Rule 5
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Observers also implement account(doc) and visibleWindow(doc, ctx). If the window is null, the pass is DEGRADED (`window.unknown`) and sends no coverage. A window without an offset, or one that ends before it starts, is DEGRADED (`window.invalid`). A window may end after the pass time, because a calendar week shows days still to come — Hard Rules 5, 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] What a pass reports. DEGRADED health: the health report only; nothing is extracted. extract() throws, returns malformed rows, or returns a type its source does not serve: DEGRADED with no observations, because a broken extractor's rows are not trusted. project() refuses a row (RedactError): DEGRADED as `redact.<code>`, the rows it accepted are still posted, and no coverage is sent for the window. Account or window unreadable: DEGRADED, rows posted, no coverage. Why: positive evidence can still verify a claim, but a window is never reported as seen when a row in it was refused (Sprint 1 close, SEC-3) — Hard Rules 5, 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Pass reason codes are a closed list, PASS_REASONS: health.threw, health.invalid, extract.threw, extract.invalid, extract.foreign_type, redact.invalid_field, redact.missing_occurred_at, redact.invalid_argument, redact.threw, account.threw, account.invalid, window.threw, window.unknown, window.invalid, message.invalid. An observer's own health reasons are checked against the core HEALTH_REASON code shape (1 to 32 codes). A reason that is free text, for example quoted page text, is replaced by `health.invalid` — Hard Rules 2, 6, 10
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Each pass reads the clock once (src/clock.ts nowIso). That instant is every observation's `observedAt` (the sighting, never the background's write time), the ping's `lastSyncAt` and the health report's `checkedAt`. The observer never sets `sightedAt` (docs/grading.md §3, §10) — Hard Rules 4, 5
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Each pass posts adapter.health, then observer.observations (at most SCHEMA_LIMITS.BATCH = 500 per message), then coverage.ping, each only after the previous one was answered. A rejected post ends the pass, so coverage never follows rows that were not delivered. Every message is checked against its bus schema before the first is sent; if any fails, the pass sends only a DEGRADED `message.invalid` health report — Hard Rules 2, 5, 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Every pass posts its health, OK included, so the stored health is always the latest and a page that recovers clears its DEGRADED banner — Hard Rule 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Passes run one at a time per observer (src/observers/serial.ts); any number of requests during a pass become one follow-up pass that reads the page as it is then. Why: a pass may only send its next sighting after the previous one was answered, so one observer never makes the ledger refuse its own rows with `late_sighting` (docs/grading.md §3; 2.7 carry-forward) — Hard Rule 4
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Every pass re-posts all of its rows; the tab keeps no record of what it already sent. Within one pass, rows with the same contentHash are posted once. Why: the ledger upsert is idempotent, and with no delivery acknowledgement a sent-rows record could skip a row the ledger never stored while later coverage claims its window. Revisit in 2.7 once acknowledgements exist — Hard Rule 5
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Batching: OBSERVER_DEBOUNCE_MS = 500 and OBSERVER_MAX_WAIT_MS = 2000 in src/config.ts. One MutationObserver watches the whole document (childList, subtree, characterData, attributes) and never reads the mutation records. The first pass runs one debounce after start, so the initial render burst is batched; on the synthetic page the first coverage.ping arrived 508–511 ms after load — Hard Rule 3
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Account hash = SHA-256 (core hashText) of the address read by normalize.email and ASCII-lowercased; nothing else is folded, so a lookalike address is another account. A non-address makes the pass DEGRADED (`account.invalid`). No account on the page sends a ping without accountHash, which grading treats as capped coverage. The hash is unsalted, so one Google account hashes the same in Gmail and Calendar and a grant's `accounts` can bind it. The address never crosses the bus — Hard Rules 2, 10 (the hash stays local, Hard Rule 1)
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] userActive = the tab is visible and trusted user input was seen within USER_ACTIVE_WINDOW_MS (60 s). Counted events: keydown, pointerdown, pointermove, wheel, touchstart. Input is noted at most once per USER_ACTIVITY_THROTTLE_MS (1 s). The listeners are passive and capture-phase, read only `isTrusted`, keep only the time of the last input, and never call preventDefault. `false` means 'no input seen here', never idle evidence; Sprint 3.5 turns gaps into idle intervals (docs/grading.md §7) — Hard Rules 3, 7
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] src/observers/text.ts oneLine() is the extractors' whitespace collapse: runs of JavaScript \s plus U+0085 become one space, then the text is trimmed. Every other control character is kept, so project() still refuses the row and the pass is DEGRADED (Sprint 1 close, SEC-3) — Hard Rules 2, 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] assertObserver runs at start. id, vendor and version must be ComponentId tokens; source an ObservationSource; hosts a non-empty subset of OBSERVER_HOSTS; and all four members functions. Otherwise it throws ObserverError('invalid_observer'). Batching options that cannot work throw ObserverError('invalid_options'). These are developer errors and fail before any page is read — Hard Rules 6, 8
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] The skeleton logs nothing and adds no LOG_EVENTS. Every failure while reading a page becomes a DEGRADED health report with a reason code, which is the loud channel the popup shows — Hard Rules 6, 10
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Observer members are typed as function properties, not method signatures, so the skeleton can hold them unbound; an object-literal method still satisfies them. This is a type-level change only, not a change to the §7 contract
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Test-only harness: tests/e2e/observer-skeleton.spec.ts bundles tests/e2e/support/observer-harness.ts in memory with Vite (one IIFE) and injects it into hand-written synthetic pages, tests/fixtures/pages/_harness/synthetic-observer{,-drafts,-refused}.html. A recording stand-in for browser.runtime sits next to it, so the real src/bus send() checks every message. Nothing is added to the build: no 127.0.0.1 host, no test entrypoint. built-bundle.spec.ts fails if any shipped file contains the harness marker, or if any host, content-script match or web-accessible resource reaches the fixture server. Limit: this proves the skeleton in a real Chromium page, not the content-script isolated world plus a real service worker; 2.7's E2E covers that — Hard Rules 3, 8, 9
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Task 2.0 makes no real captures (founder captures pending). The harness fixtures are synthetic hand-written structure named synthetic-*.html, and only roles, data-* attributes and structure are read — fixtures-first rule
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] Only a Calendar window may end after the pass instant. A gmail.sent or gmail.receipt visible window that ends after ctx.observedAt makes the pass DEGRADED with `window.future`: its rows are still posted, but no coverage is sent. This narrows the earlier [2.0] decision ('a window may end after the pass time') to gcal.event. Why: docs/grading.md §3 ends a list window at lastSyncAt or at the newest visible row. §6.1 builds coverage from the union of intervals, but graceElapsed reads the sync time of any eligible entry. A list window that claims the future would let a later ping finalise NOT_FOUND for a span nobody looked at. Instants are compared as epoch ms, so the offset and DST do not matter — Hard Rules 5, 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] PASS_REASONS gains `window.future`, so the closed list now has 16 codes — Hard Rules 2, 6
- 2026-10-03 [2.0] A page Chrome prerenders is not read. While `document.prerendering` is true the skeleton drops every pass request. On `prerenderingchange` it pokes the batcher once, so the first pass runs OBSERVER_DEBOUNCE_MS after the user opens the page. disconnect removes the listener. Why: Chrome injects content scripts into prerendered pages, and a Gmail or Calendar view the user never opened must yield neither observations nor coverage — Hard Rules 11, 5

(Hard Rule 2)
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Dexie ObservationIndex (src/ledger/index.ts): every read is an indexed `[type+timeBucket]` range plus one bucket of margin on each side, trimmed exactly to [fromMs, toMs] by core `instantMs` and ordered by (instant, id). Why: the stored bucket is computed with Date.parse and the bounds with instantMs, so the margin keeps a row whose two readings could straddle a bucket edge. Trimming makes the result independent of bucket width, as the memory index's §4.2 contract allows. Hard Rules 4, 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] The Dexie index returns rows through `readRows` (index fields stripped, strict Observation parse): `sightedAt` is kept and nothing is rebuilt from index fields (SIM-2). A corrupt row is skipped and logged by id, so it never reaches grading. Hard Rules 2, 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] `byCounterpartyToken` reads every action type's bounded `[type+timeBucket]` range and keeps rows whose core `counterpartyTokens` (§4.2) include the token exactly. It does not read the v1 `*counterpartyTokens` index: that index is still the Sprint 0 placeholder derivation, differs from §4.2, and is unbounded in time. No core path calls this method today. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Coverage intervals live in a new Dexie table `coverageIntervals` (schema v2, outbound auto-increment key, derived accountKey/startMs/endMs/syncMs). The v1 `coverage` table is not reworked, for two reasons. Dexie cannot change a primary key. And the v1 table's CoveragePing shape is the bus payload the concurrent observer base (2.0) emits, as well as the repo and export shape. The new table is not yet in LEDGER_TABLE_NAMES; registering it needs the bus enum, export and index-fields, which are outside this task's files. It holds no content. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] `recordCoverage` takes the core ObserverCoverage (with source and allCalendars). It merges a pass into every stored interval of the same (observerId, source, accountHash, allCalendars) group that it overlaps or touches, keeping the earliest start, latest end and latest lastSyncAt; a 1 ms gap is never bridged. Why: a tab open all day would otherwise add one interval per pass. The merge is sound for every batch evaluated after the write (it preserves the union seen, the group fields and the latest sync), and replay reads the coverage stored with each receipt, never this table. Hard Rules 4, 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] `coverageFor(type, window, evaluatedAt)` returns, per relevant source (§6.1), every interval synced by evaluatedAt that overlaps the window, plus each account's latest interval synced by evaluatedAt (account-less included). This meets §6.1's 'coverageFor must return all of them' (the account count) and graceElapsed (any eligible sync) at bounded size. A 300-run property proves the result decides level, caps and grace exactly as the whole store. Output is Coverage-parsed, de-duplicated and in a fixed order. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Retention rule. Cutoff C = now − 90 × 24 h, compared as instants. 1. Claims are old when claimedAt < C; receipts are old when computedAt < C. 2. A receipt and its claim always go together, iterated to a fixpoint. An UNASKED receipt superseded by a purged claim goes too (`supersededBy` holds the claim id, §8). 3. An observation goes when no kept receipt names it and it is old (occurredAt < C) or a purged receipt named it. 4. A request goes when ts < C and no kept claim names it. 5. Coverage intervals are cut at C, and split with a 1 ms hole at each purged observation's occurredAt ≥ C. Latest-ping rows go when lastSyncAt < C. 6. Grants, adapter health and meta are kept. receiptInputs (Sprint 3.6) are to be deleted with their receipts in the same transaction. It all runs in one readwrite transaction (serialised with batches; all or nothing). Why: §9's 'receipts together with their evidence and inputs'. A claim kept without its receipt would be regraded with no evidence. Evidence left behind would become a false UNASKED orphan. Coverage kept over forgotten evidence could grade a false NOT_FOUND. Hard Rules 2, 4, 5, 7.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] The purge also removes claims and requests, beyond the sprint line's 'observations and receipts'. A claim must go with its receipt (rule above). A request is user text (textHint) and dated history. Hard Rule 2.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Coverage after a purge of evidence dated after the cutoff (an old calendar claim's event next month) gets a 1 ms hole at that instant, not a cut of the whole source up to it. The hole already stops `full` coverage over that window and an orphan at that instant. Cutting would erase weeks of current Calendar coverage for one old claim. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Known limit (replay, §9): a kept receipt whose claim window reached before the cutoff may have had a non-evidence candidate there. Receipts name only their evidence, so after the purge its replay can show an inputsHash mismatch. Purging every receipt whose window overlaps a purged row would cascade without bound. Replay is exact for receipts whose claim windows lie after the cutoff. Hard Rule 4.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] RETENTION_DAYS = 90 is defined in src/ledger/retention.ts for now. src/config.ts is outside this task's files and was being edited concurrently by 2.0; moving it there is requested. Hard Rule 2.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] The purge alarm handler is `runRetentionPurge` = purgeExpired(await getLedger(), { now: nowIso() }), with the clock read at the edge. It logs nothing new, because the closed log vocabulary has no purge event and the report holds counts only. A failure rejects and handleAlarm logs alarm.handler_failed with the alarm name; the next daily alarm retries. Hard Rules 6, 10.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Schema v2 adds receipt indexes `*observationIds` (multiEntry) and `supersededBy`, so the purge finds the receipts naming an observation or superseded by a claim through an index, never a scan. IndexedDB builds them over existing rows; no data upgrade is needed.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Candidate test harness (tests/unit/ledger/index-implementations.ts): `describe.each([memory, dexie])`; only the harness changed, and all 81 expect statements are verified identical. - The Dexie implementation holds what one ledger holds (`ledgerHeld`): schema-valid rows, one per contentHash, the §4.1 representative. For honest rows this changes nothing candidates() returns. One test ledger per file is reused; a stale index read fails loud. - Two tests whose premise is rows the strict schema rejects, and the five bucketMinutes-validation cases, run on memory only. - Property oracles are computed over each implementation's held rows; their generator tallies are shared, memory first. Hard Rules 4, 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6] Purge and coverage reads leave rows that fail their schema in place. They are invisible to every read: a corrupt coverage row counts as no coverage, which is the safe direction. Hard Rules 5, 6.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6-verify] Retention purge, rule 3 'current evidence keeps its explanation' (src/ledger/retention.ts), replacing the 1 ms coverage hole of Decisions [2.6]. A receipt that names an observation with occurredAt at or after the cutoff stays, with its claim, the claim that superseded it, and those claims' receipts, repeated to a fixpoint. So no observation at or after the cutoff is ever purged, and coverage is only cut at the cutoff. Why: the hole was erased by the very next coverage pass (recordCoverage merges over it; demonstrated). After that, the purged event is either missing under full coverage (a false NOT_FOUND) or re-sighted with a first sighting of now (a false 'created' verification, or an orphan anchored now, i.e. a false UNASKED). An old calendar claim about a later event is kept until its event is itself older than the cutoff. Hard Rules 5, 7, 2.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6-verify] After rule 3, rule 2 (receipts and claims go together; an UNASKED receipt goes with the claim that superseded it) runs again from the old rows rule 3 did not keep. So a row that joined the purge only through a now-kept row stays. Example: an UNASKED receipt computed after the cutoff, superseded by an old claim that current evidence keeps. The second closure never reaches a kept row, because rule 3 keeps every row joined to a kept one. Hard Rules 2, 7.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6-verify] The purge rule in Decisions [2.6] is renumbered 1-7 as in the src/ledger/retention.ts header: 1 old, 2 pairs, 3 current evidence, 4 observations, 5 requests, 6 coverage cut at the cutoff plus latest pings, 7 receiptInputs. The [2.6] decision 'Coverage after a purge of evidence dated after the cutoff ... gets a 1 ms hole' is superseded. PurgeReport.coverageCut now counts only intervals moved to start at the cutoff. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6-verify] Coverage merge soundness bound (src/ledger/coverage.ts header). A merged interval decides exactly as its parts for every batch evaluated at or after the latest sync it merged. A batch stamped before a pass synced, whose transaction begins after the pass was stored, sees less coverage (never more), so it can only grade towards UNVERIFIABLE or miss an orphan the next batch finds. Accepted as the safe direction. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] [2.6-verify] The purge forgets accounts seen only before the cutoff, so §6.1's account count is taken over the retention window (documented in src/ledger/retention-coverage.ts). Raised to the founder as a contract question; see the residual issues. Hard Rules 2, 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.6] The poisoned run (pnpm test:poison) checks src/core's purity, so its candidate tests use the memory index only: vitest.poison.config.ts aliases tests/unit/ledger/index-implementations to index-implementations-memory.ts and leaves out tests/property/candidates-ledger-index.property.spec.ts. The Dexie index does I/O by design; under the guard its fake-indexeddb calls were charged to src/core's candidates(), which reaches it through the injected ObservationIndex. The main suite still runs every candidate test against both implementations, and tooling-config.spec.ts pins the exclusion to that one file. Protects Hard Rule 4 (the guard keeps checking all of src/core: 5,863 tests, 99.37% of its lines).
- 2026-10-03 [2.2] gmail.sent selectors.json version 1.0.0 is paired with the fixture folder tests/fixtures/pages/gmail/2026-10-03, all synthetic (adapter_engineer rule 3). The observer's version is the selectors.json version: id gmail.sent, vendor gmail, hosts OBSERVER_HOSTS.gmail, frozen object in src/observers/gmail/sent-observer.ts. Hard Rule 6.
- 2026-10-03 [2.2] The active view comes from Gmail's own route, the URL fragment read from doc.URL (never `location`). `#sent` and `#sent/pN` are the list (a `?compose=` suffix is ignored). `#sent/<id>` (an open conversation, or the preview pane showing one) is not_sent_list. Any other folder, label, search or an empty fragment is not_sent_folder. A non-mail.google.com https address is location.not_gmail. Why: Gmail keeps earlier views in the DOM, so the DOM alone cannot say which folder is active. Hard Rules 5, 6.
- 2026-10-03 [2.2] The nav is a cross-check only. If a nav link marked selected (tabindex=0, the fixture model) opens a folder other than Sent, the pass is not_sent_folder, because Gmail is between views. A nav that marks nothing does not block, so an unverified signal cannot silence the observer. To verify in 2.8 live smoke. Hard Rules 5, 6.
- 2026-10-03 [2.2] Only the region actually shown is read: exactly one visible role=main and exactly one visible role=grid inside it. Zero gives selector.missing.*, two or more gives selector.ambiguous.*. 'Visible' means no ancestor has the hidden attribute or computes to display:none, and the element is not visibility:hidden (read-only getComputedStyle). aria-hidden does not count, because a modal sets it on a page the user still sees. Residual risk for 2.8: in the instant after the route changes, the old view may still be shown. The 500 ms debounce and the nav cross-check reduce this but cannot remove it. Hard Rule 5.
- 2026-10-03 [2.2] Structure is checked per row in health(): one subject anchor ([data-legacy-last-message-id]) inside a gridcell, at least one recipient ([email] or [data-hovercard-id]) in a cell before it whose `email` (else `title`) is an address, and a [title]/[aria-label] time element in a cell after it, plus exactly one account address.
